quintile, so is fund size, so the gradient is not a composition
effect. The fee sort, by contrast, produces almost no
gradient in gross performance among active funds: EW gross returns range
from 0.977\% in the lowest-fee quintile to 0.943\% in the highest, and the
EW Sharpe ratio falls only from 0.62 to 0.54. High-fee active funds are
much smaller (\$591 million against \$5.8 billion in Q1) and trade more
(turnover 94\% against 49\%). In the passive group the highest-fee
quintile (mean expense ratio 1.09\%) is small, trades heavily, and has
the lowest Sharpe ratio in its panel.

\input{tables/table_port_size_alpha}

Table~\ref{tab:port_size_alpha} reports the size-quintile alphas. Among
active funds the Carhart gross alpha shows no size gradient. It is
$-0.108\%$ per annum in the smallest quintile and $-0.273\%$ in the
largest (EW), $+0.130\%$ and $-0.052\%$ (VW), and the highest alpha is in
the middle quintile ($+0.316\%$ EW); the Q5$-$Q1 spreads of $-0.165\%$
(EW) and $-0.182\%$ (VW) are small and not statistically significant.
The sign of the spread is the one implied by the fund-level diseconomies
of scale of \textcite{ChenHongHuangKubik2004} and by the
\textcite{BerkGreen2004} mechanism through which inflows erode gross
alpha, but the portfolio sort provides no evidence of a size effect.
Because the smallest quintile has the highest expense ratios, the absence
of a gross-alpha gradient means that, net of fees, small funds do worse,
which is the pattern behind the gap between the equal- and value-weighted
net alphas of Section~\ref{sec:aggregate_performance}.

\input{tables/table_port_mom_alpha}

Table~\ref{tab:port_mom_alpha} reports the momentum-quintile alphas. Under
the CAPM and the three-factor model the active Q5$-$Q1 spread is large and
significant ($+5.291\%$ and $+6.075\%$ per annum EW, significant at 5\%
and 1\%). Under the Carhart model it shrinks to $+2.278\%$ (EW) and
$+1.934\%$ (VW) and is no longer significant, and the momentum loadings
rise monotonically from $-0.24$ in Q1 to $+0.25$ in Q5. Most of the
continuation in fund returns is therefore explained by the funds'
exposure to stock-level momentum, as \textcite{Carhart1997} found; what
remains is a positive but statistically weak residual spread.

The rank-to-alpha conversion of the Psychological Premium Framework
(Section~\ref{sec:ppf}) is calibrated on a closely related sort carried
out in the rank space of the flow regressions themselves: active funds
are sorted each month into quintiles of their within-Lipper-category
trailing 12-month return rank. The equal-weighted Carhart net-alpha spread
of that sort is $+2.363\%$ per annum ($t = 2.40$); with a
midpoint-to-midpoint rank distance of 0.8 across the five quintiles, the
conversion is approximately 295 basis points of annual alpha per
rank-point. The momentum spread above implies a similar conversion (285
basis points) but is less precisely estimated. The basis-point figures
derived from either are best read as orders of magnitude.

\input{tables/table_port_fee_alpha}

Table~\ref{tab:port_fee_alpha} reports the fee-quintile alphas. Among
active funds the EW gross alpha is lower in the high-fee quintiles: it is $+0.115\%$ per
annum in the lowest-fee quintile and $-0.704\%$ in the highest, and the
Q5$-$Q1 spread of $-0.819\%$ is significant at 5\% ($-1.062\%$ and
$-0.669\%$ under the CAPM and the three-factor model, significant at
10\%). Funds that charge more earn less before fees, not more. Since net
alpha equals gross alpha minus the expense ratio, the net-of-fee alpha
falls by more than the fee: the expense-ratio difference between Q5 and
Q1 is about 1.2 percentage points per annum, and the net-alpha penalty
an investor bears for choosing the most expensive rather than the
cheapest quintile is about 2 percentage points. The VW pattern is
noisier and shows no reliable gradient: no quintile alpha is
significant, and the positive high-fee quintile ($+1.723\%$) is likely
driven by a few large funds. The fee evidence is difficult to reconcile with a
model in which fees are a price for expected gross outperformance.

\section{Activeness and the cross-section of fund alphas}
\label{sec:activeness}

\noindent The fee, size, and momentum quintile sorts of the previous
section partition the fund universe along characteristics that prior
literature has documented as predictors of cross-sectional alpha. None
of those characteristics, however, addresses the question that motivates
this dissertation directly: does deviating more from systematic factor
exposures or from a stated benchmark produce a different alpha? This
section provides that test.

The cross-sectional question contrasts three priors. Under
\textcite{BerkGreen2004}, capital flows erode net-alpha differences
across funds in equilibrium, and no activeness--alpha gradient is
predicted net of fees. \textcite{AmihudGoyenko2013} argue that the share
of fund return variance not explained by systematic factors, $1-R^2$,
captures productive idiosyncratic risk-taking by managers with
information advantages and therefore predicts alpha; under their prior
the gradient should be positive. \textcite{CremersPetajisto2009},
working with a holdings-based Active Share measure, document that the
most active funds outperform and that closet indexers underperform after
fees. The empirical question is which of these patterns the data exhibit.

\input{tables/table_activeness_chars}

Table~\ref{tab:activeness_chars} reports the characteristics of active
funds sorted each month into quintiles by lagged activeness. Highly
active funds are systematically smaller and more expensive. Under the
$1-R^2$ measure, the mean expense ratio rises from 0.87\% in Q1 (closet
indexers) to 1.30\% in Q5 (most active), mean TNA falls from \$4.1
billion to \$1.5 billion, and turnover rises from 55\% to 76\%. The mean
$1-R^2$ is 0.027 in Q1 --- the four factors explain 97\% of these funds'
return variance --- against 0.246 in Q5. The pattern is consistent with
the \textcite{CremersPetajisto2009} finding that highly active funds are
smaller and charge higher fees than closet indexers.

\input{tables/table_activeness_alpha_r2}

Table~\ref{tab:activeness_alpha_r2} reports the factor-model alphas by
$1-R^2$ quintile. The headline result concerns the closet indexers. The
lowest-activeness quintile earns a Carhart gross alpha of $-0.763\%$ per
annum EW ($t = -1.90$) and $-0.938\%$ VW ($t = -2.18$), and net alphas of
$-1.630\%$ EW and $-1.534\%$ VW, both significant at 1\%. The most
active quintile earns a gross alpha of $+0.846\%$ EW and $+0.455\%$ VW and
a net alpha that is negative but not statistically distinguishable from
zero ($-0.456\%$ EW, $-0.358\%$ VW). The EW Q5$-$Q1
spread is $+1.609\%$ per annum gross ($t = 2.00$, significant at 5\%)
and $+1.174\%$ net ($t = 1.46$); the corresponding VW spreads ($+1.392\%$
and $+1.176\%$) are of similar size but less precisely estimated.

The interpretation is direct. Closet indexers charge active fees for
portfolios whose returns are almost fully explained by passive factor
exposures, and they fail to recover those fees: they are the part of the
active industry in which the cost of active management is most clearly
not repaid. More active funds earn higher gross alphas that roughly cover
their higher fees, so that the net alpha of the most active quintile
cannot be distinguished from zero. The pattern supports the \textcite{AmihudGoyenko2013}
and \textcite{CremersPetajisto2009} priors and is at odds with a strict
reading of the \textcite{BerkGreen2004} equilibrium, in which predictable
net-alpha differences between identifiable groups of funds should not
survive; the evidence against it rests on the significantly negative net
alpha of the closet-indexer quintile rather than on the net Q5$-$Q1
spread, which is not significant.

\input{tables/table_activeness_alpha_te}

Table~\ref{tab:activeness_alpha_te} reports the corresponding sort on
tracking error. Here the pattern is different. The EW gross alphas are
flat across quintiles (Q5$-$Q1 spread $-0.300\%$, $t = -0.51$), and the
value-weighted results are dominated by the highest-tracking-error
quintile, whose VW gross alpha is $-2.287\%$ per annum ($t = -2.36$) and
net alpha $-3.221\%$ ($t = -3.33$). Tracking error conflates factor
exposure with security selection: a fund with a large market beta or a
strong size or sector tilt relative to its assigned benchmark has high
tracking error without necessarily being active in the
\textcite{AmihudGoyenko2013} sense, and the high-tracking-error quintile
carries a value-weighted market beta of 1.11 and a significant negative
value loading.
Benchmark assignments for a substantial fraction of funds are also
inferred rather than confirmed (Appendix~\ref{app:benchmark}). The
tracking-error sort is therefore a weaker test of activeness, and its
failure to replicate the $1-R^2$ gradient is noted as a qualification.

\input{tables/table_activeness_monotonicity}

A significant Q5$-$Q1 spread does not by itself imply that the alpha
gradient is monotone across all five quintiles. Following
\textcite{PattonTimmermann2010}, Table~\ref{tab:activeness_monotonicity}
reports the monotonic relation (MR) test for both activeness measures and
all four weighting and return combinations. The test statistic is the
smallest of the four inter-quintile differences
$\Delta_q = \alpha_q - \alpha_{q-1}$, and its null distribution is
obtained from a stationary bootstrap of calendar months under the
least-favourable configuration in which all quintile alphas are zero.

Under the $1-R^2$ measure, the null of no increasing pattern is rejected
for the equal-weighted portfolios, at 5\% gross of fees
($p_\uparrow = 0.028$) and at 10\% net of fees ($p_\uparrow = 0.061$):
every step up in activeness is associated with a higher alpha. The value-weighted gradients are not
monotone ($p_\uparrow = 0.48$ and $0.55$), because the value-weighted
alphas peak in the third quintile and fall back in the fourth. Under the tracking-error measure no
increasing pattern is detected; the only rejection is a decreasing
net-of-fee EW pattern ($p_\downarrow = 0.046$, significant at 5\%), the
opposite direction to the $1-R^2$ result. The MR
test therefore confirms that, for the typical active fund, alpha rises
with activeness measured by $1-R^2$, and that this gradient survives
fees, although net of fees only at the 10\% level.

\section{Performance persistence}
\label{sec:persistence}

\noindent The persistence of fund-level alpha is a distinct empirical
question from the level of average alpha. Whatever the level of average
alpha, persistence would imply that the cross-sectional
dispersion of realised returns carries forecastable signal. Under the
\textcite{BerkGreen2004} model, persistence in net alpha should not
survive, because investors should rationally move capital to exploit
it; persistence among losers indicates that capital leaves
underperforming funds only slowly.

The test design --- formation and holding windows, cohort structure,
decile construction, and the bootstrap null --- is described in
Section~\ref{sec:persistence_design}.

\begin{landscape}
\lscapenumtrue
\pagestyle{lscapeblank}%
\input{tables/table_persistence}
\end{landscape}
\lscapenumfalse
\pagestyle{plain}%

Table~\ref{tab:persistence} reports the results. In the full sample
(Panel~A), the bottom decile of formation-period $t(\hat{\alpha})$ (D10)
earns a holding-period Carhart gross alpha of $-0.136\%$ per month
(about $-1.6\%$ per annum; $t = -2.09$, parametric one-sided $p = 0.018$,
bootstrap left-tail $p = 0.040$), significant at the 5\% level. The top decile (D1) earns $-0.073\%$ per
month ($t = -1.41$), and no decile earns a positive alpha that is
distinguishable from zero. The D1$-$D10 spread of $0.063\%$ per month is
small and insignificant. Ranking on raw alpha instead of its $t$-statistic
(Panel~B) gives a similar D10 alpha ($-0.151\%$, bootstrap
$p = 0.032$), and its D1$-$D10 spread of $0.128\%$ per month is
significant at the 10\% level (bootstrap $p = 0.085$). The decile
portfolios' returns are close to normal except for the two lowest deciles,
where the Jarque--Bera test rejects at 5\% ($p = 0.037$ for D9 and
$p = 0.045$ for D10; $p = 0.075$ for the long-short spread); for these the
bootstrap $p$-values are the relevant ones.

The pattern is the left-tail asymmetry documented by
\textcite{Carhart1997}: past losers continue to lose, but past winners do
not continue to win. The two halves of this result have different
implications. Winner non-persistence is consistent with
\textcite{BerkGreen2004}, in which flows compete away the net-of-fee
benefit of any skill that investors can identify; because these are
gross alphas, the loser result holds a fortiori net of fees. Loser persistence is harder to
reconcile with that model, because investors could avoid the predictable
underperformance by leaving these funds; that they do so only slowly is a
recurring theme of the behavioral literature and of
Chapter~\ref{ch:behavioral}.

The sub-period panels show where the loser persistence comes from. Within
P1 (Panel~C, cohorts formed in 1995--2001) the bottom decile earns
$-0.290\%$ per month ($t = -7.83$, bootstrap $p = 0.002$), and several
middle deciles are also negative, reflecting the weak gross performance of
the late 1990s. Within P3 (Panel~E, cohorts formed after 2010) the bottom
decile alpha of $-0.107\%$ per month is no longer significant
(bootstrap $p = 0.148$), as returns are more uniformly weak across
deciles. The strongest pattern appears in the cross-regime panel
(Panel~F, cohorts 3 and 4, whose holding years 2006 and 2010 straddle
regime boundaries): D10 earns $-0.201\%$ per month ($t = -4.09$, bootstrap
$p = 0.020$), and the D1$-$D10 spread of $0.217\%$ per month ($t = 3.03$,
bootstrap right-tail $p = 0.049$) is the only spread in the table that is
significant at 5\% under the bootstrap. Funds ranked worst in one regime
continue to underperform when the
regime changes, while the top of the distribution does not. Because each
sub-period panel pools only two or three cohorts (24 to 36 months), these
panel-specific estimates should be read as indicative.

\input{tables/table_activeness_persistence}

Table~\ref{tab:activeness_persistence} extends the persistence analysis
along the activeness dimension, using a $10 \times 3$ bivariate sort.
Within each cohort, funds are partitioned into three terciles by
formation-window $1-R^2$, and within each tercile into ten alpha-decile
portfolios. The most informative result concerns the least active
tercile (T1). Among closet indexers, the past winners in D1 earn a
holding-period alpha of $-0.192\%$ per month ($t = -2.55$, bootstrap
$p_B = 0.016$), and the D1$-$D10 spread is \emph{negative} ($-0.079\%$ per
month, $t = -1.55$, $p_B = 0.095$, significant only at the 10\% level):
the highest-ranked closet indexers subsequently do worse than the
lowest-ranked ones. In the middle and most active terciles the spreads
are positive ($+0.113\%$ and $+0.259\%$ per month), the first not
significant ($p_B = 0.125$) and the second significant at the 10\% level
($p_B = 0.083$), and no D10 alpha is significant at the 5\% level (the
middle-tercile D10 alpha of $-0.136\%$ has $p_B = 0.067$).

The reversal among closet indexers is consistent with the cross-sectional
activeness evidence. A fund whose returns are almost entirely explained
by factor exposures can achieve a high formation-period
$t(\hat{\alpha})$ only through a small and largely accidental deviation
from those exposures, which is unlikely to repeat; its fee, meanwhile,
continues to be charged. Formation-period rank therefore carries little
information about skill among the least active funds, and what it
carries points the wrong way. Among more active funds the point estimates
are consistent with some persistence, which reaches the 10\% level in the
most active tercile, but the evidence is too imprecise to support a firm
conclusion in either direction.

\section{Sub-period analysis}
\label{sec:subperiod}

\noindent The rolling alpha series of Figure~\ref{fig:rolling_alphas}
exhibits visible non-stationarity. The Bai--Perron structural break test,
reported in full in Appendix~\ref{app:subperiod}, is applied to the
cross-sectional mean of the per-fund 36-month rolling gross alphas of
active funds. The BIC selects seven breaks, dated (at the end of the
rolling window) March 2000, March 2003, March 2006, August 2010, December
2015, January 2019 and January 2022; the more conservative criterion of
\textcite{LiuWuZidek1997} selects five. The regime means describe a
sequence of large swings in the first half of the sample --- $-0.59\%$,
$+2.60\%$, $-0.54\%$ and $+1.03\%$ per annum for the four segments ending
in August 2010 --- followed by four segments that are all at or below
zero ($-0.17\%$, $-0.69\%$, $-0.13\%$ and $-1.01\%$).

The most consequential break is August 2010 (95\% confidence interval November
2009--May 2011), which separates the last positive regime from the
weaker period that follows; the regime mean falls by about 1.2
percentage points. Two cautions apply to the break dates. First, because
a rolling-window break is dated at the end of the window, the change in
performance that it records may have begun up to 35 months earlier.
Second, when the same test is applied to the non-overlapping monthly
abnormal return of the equal-weighted active portfolio, the BIC selects
no break at all, and the single-break sup-$F$ test, significant only at
the 10\% level ($p = 0.074$), places the break in August 2019. The rolling-alpha breaks
are therefore best understood as a description of a deterioration that
is visible in smoothed data, rather than as precisely dated events. They
are used here only to define sub-periods, and whether the deterioration
is statistically reliable is tested directly in
Sections~\ref{sec:break_terciles} and \ref{sec:same_sample}.

The sub-period analysis uses two of the break dates as boundaries: P1
(January 1995--March 2006, 135 months), P2 (April 2006--August 2010, 53
months), and P3 (September 2010--January 2026, 185 months). The earlier
breaks of 2000 and 2003 define segments of only 36 months and are pooled
into P1, and the post-2010 breaks are pooled into P3 because every
post-2010 segment has a regime mean at or below zero and because those
breaks are very imprecisely dated (the confidence intervals of the
December 2015 and January 2019 breaks each span more than six years and
overlap). The three periods
differ sharply.

In P1 and P2, aggregate active performance is unremarkable and the
cross-section is consistent with luck. The EW gross alpha is $-0.13\%$
(P1) and $+0.46\%$ (P2) per annum, and the EW net alpha $-1.36\%$
(significant only at 10\%) and $-0.70\%$; no bootstrap percentile is distinguishable
from zero skill. The false-discovery decomposition at $\gamma^* = 0.45$
gives a different picture in the two periods. In P2 it finds a skilled
minority ($\hat{\pi}^+_A = 15.6\%$) and few unskilled funds
(4.3\%). In P1 the skilled and unskilled shares are equal (8.1\% each):
the cross-section of 1995--2006 contains both tails, and the unskilled
tail consists largely of funds that closed before 2012 and that a
survivor-only sample would not contain: among the 792 funds observed in
all three sub-periods the P1 unskilled share is 0.1\%
(Table~\ref{tab:same_sample_skill}), and under the Bloomberg-flag-only
classification the P1 EW net alpha is $-0.72\%$ rather than $-1.36\%$
(Table~\ref{tab:classification_robustness}).

In P3 the picture reverses. The EW gross alpha is $-0.745\%$ per annum
($t = -1.85$), and net of fees the EW and VW alphas are $-1.81\%$
($t = -4.49$) and $-0.99\%$ ($t = -3.19$), both significant at 1\%. The
left tail of the cross-section is worse than luck at the 1st, 5th and 10th
percentiles (Prob.\ Luck 2.8\%, 3.9\% and 3.8\%), as is the median (4.2\%), and the right tail
is compressed below its simulated counterpart. The decomposition finds an
unskilled share of 24.8\% and no detectable skilled share
($\hat{\pi}^+_A$ is negative, $-5.4\%$). Passive funds also earn
negative EW alphas in P3 ($-0.57\%$ gross, significant at 5\%, and
$-0.98\%$ net, significant at 1\%),
which suggests that part of the post-2010 shortfall reflects the
factor model's difficulty in pricing the period's returns; but active
funds fall short of passive funds by about 0.7--0.8 percentage points net
of fees on both weightings (0.83 EW, 0.71 VW), roughly the fee differential.

Read on its own, the sub-period evidence describes a cross-section that
changes across 2010: before, a minority of managers with detectable
skill, most clearly in 2006--2010, consistent with the
\textcite{BarrasScailletWermers2010} estimates for the 1990s; after, no
detectable skilled minority and roughly a quarter of active funds
underperforming the factor benchmark before fees. The full-sample
averages of Sections~\ref{sec:aggregate_performance} and
\ref{sec:fdr_results} mix these regimes. Two questions remain before
this contrast can be read as a change in the alpha-generating process.
The first is whether the fall is statistically reliable, and where in the
cross-section it occurs; the second is whether it reflects a change in
how the same funds perform or a change in which funds are in the sample.
The two following sections address them with tests specified before
their results were seen.

\subsection{Where the deterioration occurs: break tests by past performance}
\label{sec:break_terciles}

\noindent The break test above is applied to the average of all active
funds. If the 2010 deterioration were confined to the funds that were
already underperforming, it would describe the persistence of losers
rather than a change in the opportunity set of active management. To
distinguish the two, active funds are sorted each month into terciles on
the $t$-statistic of their Carhart alpha over the preceding 36 months
(the persistence signal of Section~\ref{sec:persistence}), and the
rolling 36-month alpha of each equal-weighted tercile portfolio is
tested for a shift at August 2010, the date taken from the main test
rather than re-estimated, and for breaks at unknown dates. The rule fixed
before the test was run classifies the deterioration as broad if all
three terciles show a significant fall at 5\%, as confined to the left
tail if only the bottom tercile does, and as mixed otherwise.

\input{tables/table_breaktest_terciles}

Table~\ref{tab:breaktest_terciles} reports the result, which is mixed in
the sense of the rule, and informative in its pattern. The fall is
largest in the top tercile ($-1.70$ percentage points, $t = -3.04$) and in
the middle tercile ($-1.30$, $t = -3.42$), both significant at 1\%, and
both series show a break at an unknown date close to August 2010
(sup-$F$ $p < 0.001$). In the bottom tercile the fall is smaller
($-0.73$, $t = -1.75$, significant only at 10\%) and the sup-$F$ test
finds no break ($p = 0.37$). The difference between the bottom and the
top tercile narrows by 0.97 percentage points ($t = 2.01$). The 2010
deterioration is therefore not a deepening of the left tail: it is the
loss of the outperformance of the funds that had been doing well, which
is the pattern predicted by an erosion of the opportunities available to
skilled managers rather than by the continued underperformance of poor
ones. Two qualifications apply. The tests are run on rolling-window
series, whose overlap the Newey--West correction with 35 lags addresses
only approximately, so the $t$-statistics are likely to overstate the
precision of the shifts; and the series start in December 1998, so the
breaks of 2000 lie outside the testable range. Break dates and
confidence intervals for each series are reported in
Appendix~\ref{app:subperiod}.

\subsection{Regime change or composition: the same funds across regimes}
\label{sec:same_sample}

\noindent The fund population changes across the 2010 boundary: funds
close, and new funds enter. The sub-period alphas of all active funds
therefore compare different sets of funds, and a fall in the aggregate
could reflect the replacement of better funds by worse ones rather than
a deterioration in the performance of a given fund. To separate the two,
the aggregate alphas are re-estimated on fixed samples: the 1{,}066 active
funds with at least 24 monthly returns in both P2 and P3, and the 794
funds with at least 24 in all three regimes. Because these funds survived
across the boundary, their alpha levels are biased upward relative to the
full cross-section, and the change, not the level, is the object of
interest. The change from P2 to P3 is tested in one stacked regression
with regime-specific intercepts and factor loadings.

\input{tables/table_same_sample_alpha}

Table~\ref{tab:same_sample_alpha} reports the result. For all active
funds the equal-weighted net alpha falls by 1.10 percentage points from
P2 to P3, but the fall is not statistically significant ($t = -1.42$,
$p = 0.15$); the value-weighted fall of 0.29 points is smaller and not
significant either. For the same funds observed in both regimes the
equal-weighted net alpha falls by 1.42 points ($t = -1.80$, $p = 0.072$),
and by 1.44 points for the funds observed in all three. The within-fund
change is thus larger than the change in the full cross-section, and the
composition component is positive: the funds that entered after 2010 did
somewhat better than the funds they joined, offsetting part of the
decline. By the rule fixed before the test (a within share above
two-thirds), the fall is predominantly a change in how the same funds
perform rather than a change in composition. The same funds also show the
change in the cross-section of skill: their skilled share at
$\gamma^* = 0.45$ is 18.6\% in P2 and nil in P3, while their unskilled
share rises from 0.2\% to 20.8\% (Table~\ref{tab:same_sample_skill} in
Appendix~\ref{app:subperiod}).

The two tests together qualify the regime reading of the sub-period
evidence. The deterioration after 2010 is concentrated among the funds
with better past performance and is not an artefact of a changing fund
population. Its size in the equal-weighted aggregate, about 1.1 to 1.4
percentage points per annum, is however estimated with too little
precision on 53 months of P2 data to be statistically significant at
the 5\% level on monthly returns (the same-funds changes reach only the
10\% level), and it is barely visible in the
value-weighted aggregate. The evidence supports a change in the
cross-section of skill, sharper among the better funds, more firmly than
a change in the average. Whether the change reflects the growth of
passive competition, the exhaustion of the opportunities that active
managers had exploited, or other forces is taken up in
Chapter~\ref{ch:discussion}.

\section{Dollar value added}
\label{sec:value_added}

\noindent Alpha measures the return an active fund earns per dollar;
\textcite{BerkVanBinsbergen2015} argue that skill should instead be
measured as the dollar value a manager extracts from markets, the gross
alpha multiplied by the assets managed, because in the
\textcite{BerkGreen2004} equilibrium a skilled manager grows until the
net alpha is zero, so that skill shows up in size and not in returns.
Table~\ref{tab:value_added} applies their measure, with two differences
that the table note documents: the benchmark is the Carhart factor
portfolios rather than tradable index funds, and the factor loadings are
estimated over each fund's full history.

\input{tables/table_value_added}

The life-weighted average active fund adds about \$11.6 million per year
in 2025 dollars before fees ($t = 2.85$), a figure of the same order as
the \$3.2 million in 2000 dollars that \textcite{BerkVanBinsbergen2015}
report for 1977--2011. The distribution is very skewed: the median fund
destroys a small amount (\$0.4 million) and only 32\% of funds add
positive value, so the mean reflects a minority of large funds. In the
aggregate the active industry adds \$11.3 billion per year before fees,
but the estimate is not statistically different from zero ($t = 1.28$).
Net of fees, the aggregate value delivered to investors is negative
($-\$3.4$ billion per year) and also insignificant. By regime, value
added per fund is largest in P1 (\$47 million, $t = 3.18$) and falls to
\$7.8 million in P2 and \$6.7 million in P3; net of fees, the average fund
delivers a significantly negative \$6.4 million per year to its
investors after 2010.

The dollar measure thus partly supports the
\textcite{BerkVanBinsbergen2015} reading: the average fund extracts
positive value before fees, which is what a skilled manager in the
\textcite{BerkGreen2004} equilibrium does, while investors in the
aggregate receive none of it. It does not support it in full, because
the aggregate value added is not distinguishable from zero and most
funds add none. The measure also falls after 2006, in line with the
alpha evidence.

% =============================================================================
% CHAPTER 5 --- BEHAVIORAL HYPOTHESES AND TESTS
% =============================================================================
\chapter{BEHAVIORAL HYPOTHESES AND TESTS}
\label{ch:behavioral}

\noindent The empirical chapter preceding this one establishes four facts
that constrain any behavioral interpretation of the active management
puzzle. First, before fees the aggregate active portfolio earns a Carhart
alpha close to zero, and net of fees it falls short by approximately the
cost of active management ($-1.23\%$ EW and $-0.67\%$ VW per annum).
Second, the cross-section of skill changes across 2010: a skilled
minority is detectable before 2010 and not afterwards, when the aggregate
net alpha is $-1.81\%$ per annum (EW) and about a quarter of active funds
are unskilled before fees; the fall is concentrated among funds with
better past performance and appears among the same funds, although the
change in the aggregate is not statistically significant. Third, within
the cross-section, closet indexers earn significantly negative net alphas
while the net alpha of the most active funds cannot be distinguished from
zero, and the alpha gradient in activeness survives fees (net of fees at
the 10\% level). Fourth, past
losers continue to lose while past winners do not continue to win. The
first fact is what the rational benchmark of \textcite{BerkGreen2004}
and the equilibrium accounting of \textcite{FamaFrench2010} lead one to
expect; the last three describe capital that remains in identifiably
poor parts of the industry. Whether investor behavior can explain why
that capital stays is the subject of this chapter.

This chapter tests four behavioral hypotheses against the
\textcite{BerkGreen2004} rational null on the active-fund subset of the
incubation-corrected panel (January 1998--September 2025) using the
\textcite{SirriTufano1998} piecewise rank specification with sentiment,
margin-debt, and lottery-proxy interactions. The
\textcite{Petersen2009} two-way clustering on fund and calendar month is
applied throughout. Each hypothesis is identified either within fund
using fund fixed effects (H1, H2, H3) or within style and month using
Lipper-category $\times$ year-month fixed effects (H4, where the
time-invariant expense ratio would otherwise be absorbed by fund fixed
effects). Section~\ref{sec:H1} reports the sentiment-convexity
hypothesis (H1); Section~\ref{sec:H2} the disposition /
illusion-of-control hypothesis (H2); Section~\ref{sec:H3} the lottery
demand horse race (H3); Section~\ref{sec:H4} the fee-elasticity
hypothesis (H4); and Section~\ref{sec:ppf} the Psychological Premium
Framework that translates the H3 estimates into welfare-relevant alpha
basis points. Section~\ref{sec:sent_or_unc} asks whether the H1--H3 state effects
and the Psychological Premium survive a control for macroeconomic
uncertainty, Section~\ref{sec:regime_behavioral} asks whether the effects
differ before and after the August 2010 break, and
Section~\ref{sec:behavioral_summary} synthesises the four-hypothesis
evidence.

\section{Panel regression specification}
\label{sec:panel_spec}

\noindent The dependent variable is the calculated proportional fund
flow $\text{Flow}_{i,t}$ defined in Equation~\ref{eq:flow},
winsorised at the 1st/99th percentiles and excluding December
observations. The principal regressor is the lagged Sirri--Tufano
fractional rank within Lipper category, partitioned into three
piecewise segments $R^{\text{LOW}}_{i,t-1}$, $R^{\text{MID}}_{i,t-1}$,
and $R^{\text{HIGH}}_{i,t-1}$. The rank is computed from the cumulative
12-month net return ending in $t-1$ --- the return investors actually
observe --- so that flow in month $t$ responds to the performance
information available at the start of the month, consistent with the
\textcite{SirriTufano1998} timing convention.
Controls $X_{i,t}$ include $\log(\text{TNA}_{i,t-1})$,
$\log(\text{Age}_{i,t})$, the trailing 36-month return volatility, and
the leave-one-out Lipper-category mean flow (a within-style demand
control that excludes the focal fund). The category mean flow absorbs
the aggregate flow shocks within style that would otherwise be omitted-variable bias
for the H1--H3 hypotheses.

The aligned regression sample after the lag, fixed-effect, and
state-variable joins comprises 322{,}162 fund-month observations on
2{,}311 unique active funds across 22 Lipper styles and 306 calendar
months between January 1998 and September 2025 (322{,}150 after the
removal of fixed-effect singletons). Two features of this window follow
from the construction of the regressors rather than from a separate
sample choice. First, December observations are excluded by construction;
January observations are retained, because the style-flow control for
January is built from the December flows of the style's other funds,
which are computed before the December exclusion is applied. Second, the
window ends in September 2025 although the Baker--Wurgler series runs to
December 2025: $\mathrm{SENT}^{\perp}$ is unavailable for October 2025,
its one-month lag is therefore missing for November 2025, and December
is excluded. Fund-months with lagged TNA below \$5 million are dropped
after all lags have been constructed. Robustness specifications under the
Lipper-category $\times$ year-month fixed-effect structure and with
lagged state variables are reported in
Appendix~\ref{app:behavioral_robustness}.

\section{H1: Sentiment-convexity hypothesis}
\label{sec:H1}

\noindent H1 predicts that high-sentiment regimes amplify the
flow--performance convexity. The rational null is that the
sentiment-rank-segment interactions $\delta_q$ are jointly zero across
$q \in \{\text{LOW}, \text{MID}, \text{HIGH}\}$.
Table~\ref{tab:H1_regression} reports the panel regression results under
three sentiment proxies: the Baker--Wurgler high-sentiment regime
indicator $D^{\text{SENT}}$, the orthogonalised continuous Baker--Wurgler
sentiment $\text{SENT}^{\perp}$ (standardised), and the AAII
bull-minus-bear spread regime indicator $D^{\text{AAII}}$.

\input{tables/table_H1_regression}

The baseline specification in column (1) confirms the flow convexity
documented by \textcite{SirriTufano1998}. The slope on $R^{\text{HIGH}}$
(0.0865) is more than twice the slope on $R^{\text{LOW}}$ (0.0380) and more
than five times the slope on $R^{\text{MID}}$ (0.0156), all significant at
1\%: funds in the top quintile of their style gain far more flow per unit
of rank improvement than funds elsewhere in the distribution. The
middle-segment slope is the flattest, which indicates that investors
react more strongly to rank changes at the bottom of the distribution
than in the middle.

The joint $\chi^2_3$ test of
$\delta_{\text{LOW}} = \delta_{\text{MID}} = \delta_{\text{HIGH}} = 0$
rejects the rational null at 1\% under the AAII regime indicator
($\chi^2 = 14.08$, $p = 0.003$) and at 10\% under the continuous
Baker--Wurgler index ($\chi^2 = 7.68$, $p = 0.053$); under the
Baker--Wurgler regime indicator it does not reject ($\chi^2 = 6.15$,
$p = 0.105$). The evidence that aggregate sentiment changes the
flow--performance relationship therefore rests mainly on the survey-based
proxy; the market-based Baker--Wurgler evidence is marginal. The two
families of proxies also point in different directions.

Under the Baker--Wurgler proxies, the point estimates indicate that high
sentiment \emph{flattens} the flow--performance relationship at both ends
of the distribution, although each proxy is significant at the 5\% level
at only one end and the joint tests are at best marginal. Under
$\text{SENT}^{\perp}$, a one-standard-deviation increase in sentiment
reduces the loser-segment slope by 0.0059 ($t = -2.08$), about one sixth
of its average value, and the winner-segment slope by 0.0059, although
the latter is not significant ($t = -1.49$). Under $D^{\text{SENT}}$, the winner-segment interaction is
$-0.0194$ ($t = -2.09$), about a fifth of the winner slope, and the
loser-segment interaction is also negative and significant at 10\%
($-0.0121$, $t = -1.65$). To the
extent that it is detectable, high Baker--Wurgler sentiment thus makes
flows \emph{less} sensitive to past performance: investors penalise
losers less and reward winners less when aggregate optimism is
elevated. The asymmetry test
$\delta_{\text{HIGH}} > \delta_{\text{LOW}}$, which expresses the
winner-chasing form of H1, is not supported by either Baker--Wurgler
proxy ($z = -0.71$ and $z = +0.00$).

Under the survey-based AAII proxy the pattern is closer to the original
hypothesis. Bullish survey sentiment steepens the middle-segment slope
($\hat{\delta}_{\text{MID}} = +0.0042$, $t = 2.34$) and has a positive
winner-segment interaction that is significant at 5\%
($+0.0179$, $t = 2.03$), and the asymmetry test is significant at 1\%
($z = 2.69$, one-sided $p = 0.004$). Stated retail optimism is therefore
associated with stronger performance chasing, whereas the market-based
Baker--Wurgler index is associated with weaker performance sensitivity.
One reading of the difference is that the Baker--Wurgler index captures
periods of broad, undiscriminating inflows into equity funds --- its main
effect on flows is positive and significant --- in which relative
performance matters less, while the AAII survey captures the optimism of
the retail investors who chase recent winners.

The marginal Baker--Wurgler result does not weaken under alternative
timing or fixed effects: under one-month-lagged sentiment the joint test
rejects at 10\% ($p = 0.050$ for $\text{SENT}^{\perp}_{t-1}$,
$p = 0.066$ for $D^{\text{SENT}}_{t-1}$, the latter stronger than its
contemporaneous counterpart) with the same negative loser- and
winner-segment interactions, and under Lipper-category $\times$
year-month fixed effects the $\text{SENT}^{\perp}$ test rejects at 5\%
($p = 0.032$), although the $D^{\text{SENT}}$ test does not ($p = 0.239$).
It remains, however, a 10\%-level result in the primary
specification. The AAII result survives the alternative fixed effects
($p = 0.003$; asymmetry $p = 0.007$) but not lagged timing ($p = 0.546$),
so it reflects contemporaneous co-movement of survey sentiment and flows
rather than a predictive relation. The two results respond in the opposite
way to a control for macroeconomic uncertainty: the Baker--Wurgler
evidence weakens and the AAII evidence does not
(Section~\ref{sec:sent_or_unc}).

The coefficients on the controls are as expected. The standalone
Baker--Wurgler sentiment indicator is positive and significant in
columns (2) and (3): fund flows are higher in high-sentiment months. Size
and age carry negative and significant coefficients in all
specifications, so flows decline as a fund grows larger and older, and
funds receive higher flows when the flow into their style as a whole
increases. Return volatility is insignificant under fund fixed effects,
because most of its variation lies between rather than within funds; the
negative and highly significant volatility coefficients in the
specifications without fund fixed effects (Tables~\ref{tab:H4_regression},
\ref{tab:H4_lagged}, \ref{tab:H1_robustness}, \ref{tab:H2_robustness},
\ref{tab:H3_robustness} and \ref{tab:H4_robustness}) confirm this.

\section{H2: Disposition effect and illusion of control}
\label{sec:H2}

H2 tests two competing predictions about how high-leverage regimes
modulate the loser-segment flow--performance slope. The baseline
loser-segment coefficient $\beta_{\text{LOW}}$ is positive: an
improvement in relative rank within the bottom segment attracts higher
flows, and a deterioration generates outflows. The interaction
coefficient $\delta^{\text{IoC}}_{\text{LOW}}$ determines whether high
margin debt \emph{steepens} or \emph{flattens} that slope. The
disposition / illusion-of-control prediction of
\textcite{ShefrinStatman1985} is $\delta^{\text{IoC}}_{\text{LOW}} < 0$:
leveraged investors are more reluctant to redeem from losers, dampening
the outflow response to rank deterioration and flattening the slope. The
\textcite{BrunnermeierPedersen2009} margin-call alternative predicts
$\delta^{\text{IoC}}_{\text{LOW}} > 0$: funding constraints force
accelerated liquidation of losing positions, steepening the slope and
amplifying the outflow response to any given rank deterioration.

\input{tables/table_H2_regression}

\noindent Table~\ref{tab:H2_regression} reports the panel regression
results under the detrended margin-debt regime indicator
$D^{\text{MD,Det}}$, the inverse put--call ratio regime indicator
$D^{\text{INV-PCR}}$, and a discriminant specification that includes the
margin-debt indicator together with the Baker--Wurgler sentiment
indicator $D^{\text{SENT}}$.

Under the headline margin-debt proxy, the joint $\chi^2_3$ test rejects
the null of no margin-debt modulation at 1\% ($\chi^2 = 19.15$,
$p < 0.001$), and all three rank-segment interactions are positive. The
loser-segment interaction is $\hat{\delta}^{\text{IoC}}_{\text{LOW}} =
+0.0169$ ($z = 2.43$), about half of the loser slope in months of
normal margin debt (0.0311 in the same column); the
one-sided $p$-value is $0.007$ for the Brunnermeier--Pedersen prediction
and $0.993$ for the disposition prediction. The winner-segment
interaction is larger still ($+0.0259$, $t = 2.80$). High margin-debt
regimes therefore \emph{steepen} the flow--performance relationship at
both ends: losers lose more flow and winners gain more flow for a given
change in rank. The margin-debt main effect is negative ($-0.0040$,
$t = -2.58$), so that the average fund receives less flow when leverage
is high.

The evidence rejects the disposition / illusion-of-control prediction in
sign. No margin-debt specification in this chapter or in
Appendix~\ref{app:behavioral_robustness} produces a negative
loser-segment interaction. The pattern is consistent with the
\textcite{BrunnermeierPedersen2009} mechanism, in which balance-sheet
constraints force faster liquidation of losing positions when leverage is
high, and more broadly with heightened performance sensitivity in
leveraged, risk-seeking markets. The finding does not falsify the
disposition effect at the individual-investor level, where it is robustly
documented by \textcite{Odean1998} and others; it indicates that the
effect does not produce a detectable \emph{aggregate} flow signature
conditional on margin-debt regimes.

The nature of the rejection matters for the question of the
dissertation. H2 rejects the \textcite{BerkGreen2004} null, in which flows
respond to performance in the same way in every state of the market, but
the mechanism the evidence points to is not a cognitive bias. Forced
deleveraging when margin debt is high is the response of investors who
face a funding constraint, and is rational given that constraint; what
it contradicts is the frictionless capital market of the baseline model,
not the rationality of investors. The H2 result is therefore read in
this dissertation as evidence of a funding-constraint friction rather
than as behavioral evidence, and it is counted separately from the
psychological channels in the synthesis of
Section~\ref{sec:behavioral_summary}. The effect is also concentrated in
the period after 2010: in the pooled regime-interaction specification,
the margin-debt interactions are jointly insignificant before August 2010
($p = 0.60$), when high margin-debt months are few, and significant
afterwards ($p < 0.001$), although the loser-segment interaction is of
similar size in both periods (0.0145 and 0.0185), and the difference
between the two periods is significant at 5\%
($p = 0.028$; Table~\ref{tab:regime_pooled}).

The inverse put--call ratio specification produces no signal: the
loser-segment interaction is negative but insignificant
($\hat{\delta} = -0.0090$, $z = -1.27$), and the joint test does not
reject ($p = 0.466$) on the shorter November 2003--October 2019 sample on
which the ratio is available. The discriminant specification preserves
the margin-debt pattern (loser segment $+0.0151$, $z = 2.15$; winner
segment $+0.0229$, $t = 2.44$; joint $\chi^2 = 16.67$, $p < 0.001$), while
the sentiment interactions are individually insignificant, so the
margin-debt effect is not an artefact of omitted sentiment variation.
The joint test also rejects at 1\% under lagged timing ($p < 0.001$) and
under Lipper-category $\times$ year-month fixed effects ($p < 0.001$,
loser-segment $z = 2.51$).

The control variables behave as in H1. Fund size and age carry negative
and significant coefficients, style flow is positive and significant,
and return volatility is insignificant under fund fixed effects in the
margin-debt specifications, for the reason noted in Section~\ref{sec:H1}
(it is significant at 10\% only on the shorter put--call sample).

\section{H3: Lottery demand and skewness preference}
\label{sec:H3}

H3 predicts that high-sentiment regimes amplify investor demand for funds
with lottery-like return distributions. The specification regresses fund
flow on two candidate lottery proxies and an activeness control, each
interacted with the sentiment regime indicator: $\text{ActR2}$ (the
lagged rolling $1-R^2$, the rational alpha-prediction channel of
\textcite{AmihudGoyenko2013}, which is not a lottery feature),
$\text{ActSkew}$ (sample skewness of the trailing 36 monthly net
returns, the full-distribution lottery measure), and $\text{MAX12}$ (the
maximum monthly net return over the trailing 12 months, the
right-tail-only measure following \textcite{BaliCakiciWhitelaw2011} and
the fund-level extension of \textcite{ChengEtal2025}). Each proxy is
winsorised at the 1st/99th percentiles, standardised within the
estimation sample, and lagged one month, so that coefficients are flow
responses per standard deviation. The proxy validation procedure that
selects these three from the four candidates considered is documented in
Appendix~\ref{app:proxy_validation}. The H3 sample excludes sector and
covered-call funds (Appendix~\ref{app:benchmark}) and comprises 273{,}497
fund-months on 1{,}997 funds (273{,}486 after the removal of fixed-effect
singletons).

\input{tables/table_H3_regression}

Table~\ref{tab:H3_regression} reports the four-column horse race.
Column (1) is the baseline Sirri--Tufano specification without any
lottery proxy. Columns (2)--(4) add each of the three lottery proxies in
turn, fully interacted with the Baker--Wurgler sentiment regime
indicator.

The $\text{ActR2}$ specification detects no signal: the joint
$\chi^2_2 = 1.36$ ($p = 0.508$) and the sentiment-amplification test
($z = -0.52$) are both insignificant. Activeness, whatever its value as a
predictor of alpha (Section~\ref{sec:activeness} and
Appendix~\ref{app:proxy_validation}), does not attract differential flow
in the fund fixed-effect specification.

The $\text{ActSkew}$ specification is also insignificant in the headline
specification ($\chi^2_2 = 1.48$, $p = 0.477$; $z = 0.54$, $p = 0.295$).
It becomes strongly significant under Lipper-category $\times$ year-month
fixed effects ($\chi^2_2 = 40.00$, $p < 0.001$; $z = 2.52$, $p = 0.006$),
where identification comes from differences across funds within a style
and month rather than from changes within a fund over time. Investors
appear to favour funds whose return distributions are more positively
skewed than their peers', particularly in high-sentiment months, but a
given fund's flows do not track changes in its own skewness.

The $\text{MAX12}$ specification produces the behavioral signature in the
headline specification. The joint test rejects at 5\%
($\chi^2_2 = 7.57$, $p = 0.023$), and the sentiment-amplification
coefficient is positive ($+0.0014$ per standard deviation, $t = 2.06$;
one-sided $p = 0.020$), while the main effect of $\text{MAX12}$ is close
to zero. High-sentiment regimes amplify flow into funds with extreme
recent maximum returns; in low-sentiment months the same characteristic
attracts no detectable flow. The finding is consistent with the
\textcite{BaliCakiciWhitelaw2011} evidence that investors overweight
extreme positive returns at the stock level, with the probability
weighting of \textcite{BarberisHuang2008}, and with the fund-level
evidence of \textcite{ChengEtal2025}. The amplification survives lagged
sentiment ($z = 1.95$, $p = 0.026$; joint $p = 0.032$). Under the
Lipper-category $\times$ year-month fixed effects the $\text{MAX12}$ main
effect becomes positive and significant (joint $p = 0.032$) but the
sentiment amplification does not ($z = 0.00$), so the cross-sectional
and within-fund identifications locate the lottery effect in different
coefficients. The sentiment main effect remains positive in all three
lottery columns, significant at 10\%, so $\text{MAX12}$ does not
absorb the time-series variation in flows that the sentiment indicator
captures.

Three limits of the H3 evidence should be stated plainly. First, the
behavioral signature rests on $\text{MAX12}$ alone: of the three proxies,
only $\text{MAX12}$ rejects the null in the headline specification, and
the skewness proxy does so only across funds within a style and month.
Second, the result depends on the fund fixed effects. Added in a fixed
order, the regressors produce no significant MAX12 amplification until
the fund effects are included (joint $p = 0.41$ with the interactions
alone and $0.50$ with the controls, against $0.023$ with fund fixed
effects; Table~\ref{tab:stepwise} in
Appendix~\ref{app:behavioral_robustness}): the effect is a within-fund
response to changes in a fund's own maximum return, which pooled
comparisons across funds with persistently different return
distributions mask. Third, in the pooled regime-interaction
specification the $\text{MAX12}$ terms are jointly insignificant both
before and after August 2010 ($p = 0.82$ and $0.18$; amplification
$z = 0.38$ and $1.03$), and they do not differ between the two periods
($p = 0.98$; Table~\ref{tab:regime_pooled}). Both sub-period estimates of
the amplification (0.0006 and 0.0007) are about half the full-sample
estimate, so part of the full-sample effect is identified from
differences between the two regimes rather than within either, and the
result is not robust to letting all behavioral coefficients differ
across regimes. It is not an effect
confined to one regime.

\section{H4: Fee elasticity}
\label{sec:H4}

H4 tests whether flow--fee elasticity is heterogeneous across performance
segments and sentiment regimes in a manner inconsistent with the rational
benchmark. Under the \textcite{BerkGreen2004} rational equilibrium, fee
sensitivity should be invariant to sentiment regime: the unconditional
fee--sentiment interaction $\eta$ and the three rank-conditioned triple
interactions $\delta^F_{\text{LOW}}$, $\delta^F_{\text{MID}}$, and
$\delta^F_{\text{HIGH}}$ should be jointly zero. The behavioral
alternative permits heterogeneity: \textcite{DelGuercioTkac2002}
document that retail and institutional clienteles evaluate managers
differently, and \textcite{ChoiLaibsonMadrian2010}
document that even sophisticated investors fail to minimise fees among
otherwise-identical S\&P 500 index funds. The behavioral prediction is
therefore a non-zero joint $\chi^2$ on the triple interactions.

\begingroup
\captionsetup{font=footnotesize, justification=raggedright, singlelinecheck=false}  % T2.11: match caption size to longtable body (\fontsize{9}{11}) and left-align
\input{tables/table_H4_regression}
\endgroup

\noindent Table~\ref{tab:H4_regression} reports the H4 regression under
three sentiment proxies. In the baseline column (1) the expense ratio has
a negative and highly significant effect on flows
($\hat{\theta} = -0.0041$, $t = -5.52$): with flows measured as a
fraction of assets per month, a fund that charges one percentage point
more receives about 0.4 percentage points of assets less inflow per
month, conditional on performance rank and the other controls. Investors
are, on average, fee-sensitive.

In columns (2)--(4) the main effect $\theta$ is smaller and less
precisely estimated (insignificant in column (2), significant at 5\%
in columns (3) and (4)), because the fee-rank interactions $\phi_q$ share
the fee effect with it; $\theta$ now measures the fee effect at the point
where all three rank segments equal zero, which lies outside the support
of the data. The economically relevant quantity is the total marginal fee
effect at a realistic rank, $\hat{\theta} + \sum_q \hat{\phi}_q R^q$. At a
typical winner (rank 0.85, so that $R^{\text{LOW}} = 0.20$,
$R^{\text{MID}} = 0.60$, $R^{\text{HIGH}} = 0.05$), the column (2)
estimates give $-0.0025 + (-0.0091)(0.20) + (-0.0016)(0.60) +
(0.0260)(0.05) = -0.0035$ in low-sentiment months, and even at the very
top of the distribution (rank 1.0) the marginal effect is essentially
zero ($-0.0001$). Higher fees reduce flows throughout the support of the
rank distribution except at its very top, where the fee penalty
vanishes.

The joint $\chi^2_4$ test of $\eta = \delta^F_{\text{LOW}} =
\delta^F_{\text{MID}} = \delta^F_{\text{HIGH}} = 0$ is insignificant under
all three proxies: $\chi^2 = 6.63$ ($p = 0.157$) under $D^{\text{SENT}}$,
$\chi^2 = 3.02$ ($p = 0.555$) under $\text{SENT}^{\perp}$, and
$\chi^2 = 3.07$ ($p = 0.547$) under $D^{\text{AAII}}$. The unconditional
fee--sentiment interaction $\hat{\eta}$ is likewise insignificant under
every proxy; under $D^{\text{SENT}}$ its point estimate is negative
($z = -1.52$), so that, if anything, fee sensitivity tightens rather than
weakens in high-sentiment months, the opposite of the H4 prediction. The same conclusion holds under lagged state
variables (joint $p$ between 0.421 and 0.967) and under year-month fixed
effects (joint $p$ between 0.186 and 0.595). H4 is therefore consistent
with the \textcite{BerkGreen2004} rational null: fee sensitivity does not
weaken in high-sentiment regimes, either on average or within any rank
segment.

One lower-order interaction merits comment. The winner-segment fee
interaction $\phi_{\text{HIGH}}$ ($R^{\text{HIGH}} \times
\text{ExpRatio}$) is positive and significant in all three columns
($t$ between 2.22 and 3.19; at 5\% or better in all three
columns): among top-performing funds, higher fees
attenuate, but do not reverse, the fee penalty. The loser-segment
interaction $\phi_{\text{LOW}}$ is negative but insignificant. A natural
reading is the search-cost channel of \textcite{SirriTufano1998}, who
find that the flow--performance relation is most pronounced for funds
with higher marketing effort, measured by higher fees. A second, not
mutually exclusive, reading is that a high-fee fund that nevertheless
reaches the top of its style has cleared a larger gross-alpha hurdle,
from which investors may infer that it is more skilled. Because the corresponding
triple interactions with sentiment are insignificant at 5\% under every
proxy, this pattern does not vary with sentiment and does not threaten
the H4 null.

The negative result for H4 is a clean inferential outcome, not a failed
test. It gives no support to sentiment-contingent fee blindness as a
mechanism: the state effects detected in H1--H3 do not operate through
investors becoming inattentive to costs when optimism is elevated. It
also sits alongside the portfolio-sort evidence of
Section~\ref{sec:portfolio_sorts} in an instructive way: investors
penalise high fees in their flow decisions, yet high-fee funds do not
earn higher gross alphas. Fee sensitivity is present but not strong
enough to eliminate the funds whose fees are not repaid.

\section{The psychological premium framework}
\label{sec:ppf}

The H3 finding that $\text{MAX12}$ attracts flow in high-sentiment
regimes carries an implication beyond the hypothesis test itself:
investors are revealed to be willing to trade expected performance for
lottery exposure, and the magnitude of this trade-off can be expressed in
welfare-relevant units. The Psychological Premium Framework introduced in
this section computes the marginal rate of substitution between
performance rank and the lottery proxy implied by the flow indifference
condition, and converts this rate into basis points of annual Carhart
alpha that investors are revealed to be willing to forgo for an
incremental standard deviation of lottery exposure.

The construction follows three steps. First, the Sirri--Tufano piecewise
rank specification of H3 is re-estimated separately for each rank
segment, with the segment, the lottery proxy, and both of their
interactions with the sentiment regime indicator. Second, the marginal
rate of substitution at a given segment is computed as the ratio of the
partial derivative of flow with respect to the lottery proxy to the
partial derivative of flow with respect to the rank within that segment:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:mrs}
\text{MRS}_q = -\left.\frac{\partial \text{Flow} / \partial \text{Lottery}}{\partial \text{Flow} / \partial R^q}\right|_{X = \bar{X}}.
\end{equation}
Third, the marginal rate of substitution is converted into alpha basis
points using a rank-to-alpha mapping estimated in the rank space of the
flow regressions themselves. Each month active funds are sorted into
quintiles of the within-Lipper-category trailing 12-month return rank
that defines $R^{\text{LOW}}$, $R^{\text{MID}}$ and $R^{\text{HIGH}}$, and
the equal-weighted quintile portfolios are held for the month. The
Q5$-$Q1 spread in Carhart net alpha is $+2.363\%$ per annum ($t = 2.40$);
with a midpoint-to-midpoint rank distance of $0.8$, this implies about
295 basis points of annual alpha per rank-point. Calibrating in the same
rank space as the regressions avoids translating between the flow rank
and a different sorting variable. The momentum sort of
Section~\ref{sec:portfolio_sorts} gives a similar conversion (285 basis
points per rank-point) from a spread that is not statistically
significant ($t = 1.36$). Even the native spread is estimated with
considerable error, so the conversion is an order-of-magnitude device
rather than a precise mapping. The premium also inherits the limits of
the H3 evidence stated in Section~\ref{sec:H3}: it rests on
$\text{MAX12}$ alone and is identified only within fund.

\input{tables/table_psychological_premium}

Table~\ref{tab:psychological_premium} reports the shadow-price estimates
for the three lottery proxies across the three rank segments and the two
sentiment regimes. The $\text{ActR2}$ and $\text{ActSkew}$ estimates are
insignificant in every segment, consistent with their headline H3
results. The $\text{MAX12}$ estimates show a clear regime contrast. At
the middle rank segment, the shadow price in low-sentiment months is
$-0.011$ rank-points per standard deviation of lottery exposure (standard
error $0.028$), indistinguishable from zero and equivalent to about 3
basis points of annual alpha. In high-sentiment months it is $-0.133$
(standard error $0.048$), equivalent to about $0.133 \times 295 \approx 39$
basis points. The high-minus-low difference of $-0.123$ rank-points (about
36 basis points) is significant at 1\% (one-sided $p < 0.01$), and the
corresponding differences at the loser and winner segments are
significant at 5\% (one-sided $p = 0.018$ and $p = 0.011$). Because the
low-sentiment shadow price is not distinguishable from zero, the regime
contrast is better expressed as this difference than as a ratio: the
lottery premium is essentially absent in low-sentiment months and
emerges in high-sentiment months.

The economic magnitude is meaningful but bounded. A premium of about
34--37 basis points of annual alpha per standard deviation of $\text{MAX12}$ is
roughly half of the net-of-fee shortfall of the aggregate active
portfolio ($-0.67\%$ VW), and about a third of the average active expense
ratio. In high-sentiment months, investors behave as if one standard
deviation of recent extreme upside were worth giving up a fund
performance rank worth about a third of a percentage point of annual
alpha. The behavioral interpretation is that part of the demand for
active funds is demand for lottery-like exposure, priced in performance
that investors would otherwise require.

\input{tables/table_behavioral_summary}

\section{Sentiment or uncertainty?}
\label{sec:sent_or_unc}

\noindent The H1 and H2 state variables are not the only aggregate
conditions that could change how flows respond to performance. In the
rational learning model of \textcite{BerkGreen2004}, investors infer
manager skill from realised returns, and the weight they place on a given
return falls when returns are less informative. Periods in which the
economy is hard to forecast could therefore flatten the flow--performance
relation without any behavioral bias, and \textcite{FranzoniSchmalz2017}
document that the sensitivity of flows to performance does vary with
aggregate market conditions. If high Baker--Wurgler sentiment tends to
occur when uncertainty is high, the dampening attributed to sentiment in
Section~\ref{sec:H1} could be a rational response to uncertainty. To
separate the two, each H1 and H2 state variable is re-estimated together
with the interactions of the three rank segments with the
\textcite{JuradoLudvigsonNg2015} uncertainty index $U$
(Section~\ref{sec:state_variables}). Dummy states are paired with the
uncertainty regime indicator $D^{\text{UNC}}$, and the continuous
Baker--Wurgler index with the standardised continuous uncertainty index.
All specifications are estimated on one common sample of 323,623
fund-months (323,610 after the removal of fixed-effect singletons;
2,313 funds, January 1998--November 2025), which differs
slightly from the primary samples because it does not require the
lagged state variables used in the timing robustness checks and
therefore keeps November 2025; as a result the statistics without $U$ in
Table~\ref{tab:unc_summary} differ marginally from those in
Tables~\ref{tab:H1_regression} and~\ref{tab:H2_regression}. The
one-month horizon and the presentation of the analysis (a summary here,
full results in Appendix~\ref{app:uncertainty}) were fixed before the
estimates were seen.

\input{tables/table_unc_summary}

Uncertainty on its own appears to change the flow--performance
relation, in the same direction as Baker--Wurgler sentiment, although the
evidence is marginal. Without a behavioral state in the model, the three
uncertainty interactions are jointly significant at 10\% ($p = 0.052$ for $D^{\text{UNC}}$ and $p = 0.054$ for the
continuous index; Table~\ref{tab:unc_horizons}), and high uncertainty
flattens the loser segment: the $R^{\text{LOW}} \times D^{\text{UNC}}$
interaction is $-0.0164$ ($t = -2.51$), about a third of the loser
slope.

Once uncertainty is controlled for, the already marginal Baker--Wurgler
evidence weakens further. Under $D^{\text{SENT}}$ the joint test does not
reject even without uncertainty ($p = 0.106$) and is far from rejecting
with it ($p = 0.330$): the loser-segment interaction
falls from $-0.0123$ to $-0.0046$ ($t = -0.60$), and only the
winner-segment interaction remains significant at 10\% ($-0.0168$,
$t = -1.76$; Table~\ref{tab:unc_H1}). Under $\text{SENT}^{\perp}$ the
joint $p$-value rises from 0.052 to 0.213 and the loser-segment
interaction shrinks by more than a third, from $-0.0060$ to $-0.0037$
($t = -1.18$). In the joint models the uncertainty interactions are not
jointly significant either ($p = 0.183$ and $0.210$), so the data cannot
attribute the common dampening to one variable rather than the other.
The reason is that the two states largely coincide: 70\% of
high-sentiment months are also high-uncertainty months (the correlation
between the two indicators is 0.43, and between the continuous indices
0.25).

The AAII and margin-debt results are unaffected. Under $D^{\text{AAII}}$
the joint test rejects at 1\% with and without uncertainty ($p = 0.003$
in both), and the asymmetry statistic is unchanged ($z = 2.72$, one-sided $p = 0.003$, without and
$z = 2.78$, $p = 0.003$, with uncertainty), as are the middle- and
winner-segment interactions ($+0.0042$, $t = 2.32$, and $+0.0175$,
$t = 1.98$; Table~\ref{tab:unc_AAII}). Under the detrended margin-debt
state the joint test still rejects at 1\% ($p < 0.001$), the
winner-segment interaction is almost unchanged ($+0.0248$, $t = 2.66$),
and the loser-segment interaction remains positive and significant at
5\% ($+0.0143$, $t = 2.05$, against $+0.0167$, $t = 2.41$,
without uncertainty; Table~\ref{tab:unc_H2}). Neither regime overlaps
with high uncertainty: the correlation of the uncertainty indicator with
the AAII indicator is $-0.02$ and with the margin-debt indicator $-0.11$.
The conclusions are the same at the three- and twelve-month horizons
(Table~\ref{tab:unc_horizons}): the AAII and margin-debt tests reject at
1\% at every horizon, the $\text{SENT}^{\perp}$ $p$-value rises to 0.234
and 0.343, and the $D^{\text{SENT}}$ $p$-value stays above 0.30.

The H3 lottery result and the Psychological Premium also condition on
the Baker--Wurgler regime indicator, so they were re-estimated in the
same way, adding $D^{\text{UNC}}$ and its interaction with the lottery
proxy (and, for the Premium, with the rank segments). This extension
was added after the H1 and H2 results above were known; it uses the
same pairing and the same one-month horizon. Unlike the Baker--Wurgler
H1 result, the
$\text{MAX12}$ amplification survives: its $z$-statistic moves from
2.06 ($p = 0.020$) to 1.95 ($p = 0.026$), the joint test of the
$\text{MAX12}$ terms weakens from $p = 0.023$ to $p = 0.062$, and
uncertainty itself does not amplify the response to $\text{MAX12}$
($z = 0.26$ without and $-0.68$ with the sentiment terms;
Table~\ref{tab:unc_H3}). The Psychological Premium at the middle rank
segment is, if anything, larger once uncertainty is held fixed: the
regime difference is $-0.134$ rank points ($p = 0.023$) against $-0.123$
($p = 0.010$) without the control, or about 7 and 47 basis points in
low- and high-sentiment months against 3 and 39. The results are the same
at the three- and twelve-month horizons.

The control for uncertainty therefore separates the H1 evidence into two
parts. The weak evidence that high Baker--Wurgler sentiment flattens the
flow--performance relation cannot be attributed to sentiment with
confidence: it is equally consistent with investors rationally
discounting performance signals when the macroeconomy is hard to
forecast, which is the learning mechanism of \textcite{BerkGreen2004}.
The stronger performance chasing associated with bullish retail survey
sentiment, by contrast, and the steeper flow--performance relation in
high-leverage regimes are not explained by this learning channel. The
behavioral content of H1 therefore rests on the AAII result --- subject
to the timing caveat of Section~\ref{sec:H1} --- rather than on the
Baker--Wurgler index, and the H2 margin-debt result stands. In the
post-hoc check for H3, the $\text{MAX12}$ amplification is not explained
by uncertainty ($z = 1.95$), although the joint test of the
$\text{MAX12}$ terms weakens to $p = 0.062$.

\section{Behavioral effects before and after 2010}
\label{sec:regime_behavioral}

\noindent Chapter~\ref{ch:results} shows that the cross-section of skill
changes across 2010. If the behavioral channels are to explain why
capital stays in poorly performing funds, it matters whether they operate
in both regimes. Each primary H1--H3 specification is therefore re-estimated
with every behavioral regressor also interacted with a dummy for the
months after August 2010, the boundary fixed by the break test; the test
and its placement were specified before the results were seen.

\input{tables/table_regime_pooled}

Table~\ref{tab:regime_pooled} reports the results. The AAII effect is
stable: its interactions do not differ between the two periods
($p = 0.73$), and the headline asymmetry is of similar size before and
after (0.026 and 0.029), although it is significant only after 2010
($z = 2.31$ against $1.31$). The Baker--Wurgler interactions behave
differently: they are jointly significant both before and after 2010
($p = 0.013$ and $p < 0.001$ for $D^{\text{SENT}}$) and change
significantly between the two periods ($p < 0.001$), with a negative
headline asymmetry before 2010 ($-0.035$, $z = -2.16$) and none after,
although the interactions remain jointly significant after 2010.
The weak full-sample Baker--Wurgler result thus averages state effects
that are not stable over time, which is a further reason not to rest the
behavioral case on it. The margin-debt interactions are jointly
significant only after 2010 (Section~\ref{sec:H2}). The $\text{MAX12}$
terms are jointly insignificant in each period on their own and do not
differ between them; both sub-period amplification estimates are smaller
than the full-sample one (Section~\ref{sec:H3}). Separate estimates for each sub-period, the same tests under
Lipper-category $\times$ year-month fixed effects, stepwise
specifications and the sensitivity to the \$5 million TNA floor are
reported in Appendix~\ref{app:behavioral_robustness}.

\section{Synthesis of behavioral evidence}
\label{sec:behavioral_summary}

\noindent Table~\ref{tab:behavioral_summary} summarises the four
hypothesis tests. In the table, the ``Joint test'' column reports whether
the test of the state-dependent terms is rejected at 10\%, and the
``Headline direction'' column names the direction of the
hypothesis-specific statistic only where its one-sided $p$-value is below
0.10. Three of the four hypotheses reject the rational null that the
state variable leaves the flow--performance relationship unchanged, but
only two of them through a psychological channel. H1 rejects through the
survey-based AAII proxy: bullish retail sentiment strengthens performance
chasing, as the hypothesis predicts, and the result survives a control
for macroeconomic uncertainty, although it reflects contemporaneous
co-movement rather than a predictive relation. The Baker--Wurgler
proxies, by contrast, give at best marginal evidence, of the opposite
sign (a flatter rather than steeper relation), that cannot be separated
from uncertainty and is not stable across regimes. H3 rejects through
$\text{MAX12}$ alone, with the qualifications stated in
Section~\ref{sec:H3}. H2 rejects the null, but in the direction of the
\textcite{BrunnermeierPedersen2009} margin-call mechanism rather than the
disposition effect: high margin debt steepens rather than flattens the
loser-segment slope. That is evidence of a funding-constraint friction,
which contradicts the frictionless baseline but not investor rationality,
and it is not counted here as behavioral evidence. H4 is consistent with
the rational null.

The picture that emerges is consistent with the hybrid empirical
position introduced in Chapter~\ref{ch:introduction}, but it is more
specific than a simple division between rational capital-weighted
outcomes and a behavioral cross-section. Investors are performance- and
fee-sensitive on average, as the rational benchmark requires, and the
aggregate active portfolio performs as equilibrium accounting implies.
What the evidence adds is that the \emph{strength} of performance
sensitivity varies with aggregate states in ways unrelated to fund
skill: it strengthens when retail survey sentiment is bullish, and in
high-sentiment regimes flows are drawn to funds with lottery-like recent
returns; it also strengthens when margin debt is high, through a funding
constraint rather than a bias. Each of these mechanisms weakens the link
between flows and expected alpha on which the Berk--Green equilibrium
relies, and each offers a channel through which
capital can remain in the poorly performing segments documented in
Chapter~\ref{ch:results} --- losers that persist, closet indexers, and
high-fee funds whose gross alpha does not cover their fees --- for longer
than the rational model implies.

% =============================================================================
% CHAPTER 6 --- DISCUSSION
% =============================================================================
\chapter{DISCUSSION}
\label{ch:discussion}

\noindent The empirical evidence assembled in Chapters \ref{ch:results}
and \ref{ch:behavioral} supports a hybrid empirical position that this
chapter develops in detail. At the aggregate level, active mutual funds
behave as equilibrium accounting and the \textcite{BerkGreen2004} model
lead one to expect: their gross alpha is close to zero and investors pay
the costs. Below the aggregate, the evidence is less comfortable for the
rational account. Skill has become rarer over time, the
underperformance concentrates in identifiable groups of funds --- closet
indexers and past losers --- and the flows that should discipline those
funds respond to aggregate states and to lottery-like characteristics in
ways the rational model does not predict.

\section{The Berk--Green equilibrium accepted at the aggregate level}
\label{sec:bg_accepted}

\noindent Over January 1995--January 2026, the aggregate active
portfolio earns a Carhart gross alpha of $-0.09\%$ per annum
equal-weighted and $+0.02\%$ value-weighted, and net alphas of $-1.23\%$
and $-0.67\%$. These estimates are close to the 1984--2006 estimates of
\textcite{FamaFrench2010} and support their central conclusion: active
funds as a group hold a portfolio that, before costs, performs like the
factor benchmark, and the costs of active management are passed on to
investors. The conclusion applies to the representative dollar as well as
to the representative fund, but not to the same degree: the
equal-weighted net alpha is about 56 basis points below the
value-weighted one, while the gross alphas differ by only 11 basis
points, so the additional shortfall of the representative fund is a
matter of costs, which are higher in the small funds that dominate the
equal-weighted average.

The \textcite{BerkGreen2004} model is consistent with the zero gross
alpha: competition for capital drives each fund to the size at which its
skill is exhausted. Its sharper prediction, a zero net alpha, is harder
to reconcile with a persistent shortfall equal to the average fee. Over
the full sample the equal-weighted net alpha rejects zero at the 1\%
level and the value-weighted net alpha at the 10\% level only, but both
are much closer to minus the expense ratio than to zero, and after 2010
the value-weighted net alpha is $-0.99\%$ per annum with a $t$-statistic
of $-3.2$. The \textcite{BerkVanBinsbergen2015} dollar-value framework
offers a complementary reading: managers can extract value from markets,
and capture it in fees, even when investors receive none of it.
Section~\ref{sec:value_added} estimates this directly. The average fund
adds about \$11.6 million per year before fees ($t = 2.85$),
while the value delivered to investors in the aggregate is negative and
insignificant; but the aggregate value added before fees is not
distinguishable from zero, and only about a third of funds add positive
value. The dollar evidence is therefore consistent with skilled managers
capturing the value they create, as \textcite{BerkVanBinsbergen2015}
argue, but only for a minority
of large funds, not for the typical one.

The H4 fee-elasticity results reinforce the aggregate reading. Flows fall
significantly with the expense ratio, and the joint test of
$\eta = \delta^F_{\text{LOW}} = \delta^F_{\text{MID}} =
\delta^F_{\text{HIGH}} = 0$ fails to reject under all three sentiment
proxies, so fee sensitivity does not weaken when optimism is elevated.
Investors are not fee-blind, and the behavioral channels documented in
Chapter~\ref{ch:behavioral} do not operate through fee processing.

\section{The cross-sectional puzzle: A behavioral account}
\label{sec:behavioral_cross_section}

\noindent If the aggregate behaves as the rational model predicts, where
does the puzzle lie? The evidence points to three parts of the active
industry in which capital earns predictably poor returns and nevertheless
remains. The first is the closet indexers: the least active quintile of
funds earns a net alpha of about $-1.5\%$ to $-1.6\%$ per annum on both weightings,
significant at 1\%, while charging active fees for portfolios whose
returns the four factors explain almost entirely. The second is past
losers: the bottom decile of past performance continues to underperform
by about 1.6\% per annum before fees. The third is high-fee funds, whose
gross alpha is no higher than that of low-fee funds --- among
equal-weighted portfolios it is significantly lower --- so that their net
alpha falls by at least as much as their fee. In each case an investor
using only public information could have avoided the shortfall. Under the
\textcite{BerkGreen2004} model, capital should leave these funds until
their expected net alpha is zero; the evidence says that it leaves too
slowly.

The behavioral evidence of Chapter~\ref{ch:behavioral} identifies three
channels that change the discipline that flows impose, two of them
behavioral and one a market friction. The sentiment channel (H1)
operates by changing the strength of performance sensitivity. Its
clearest form is survey-based: when retail investors are bullish (AAII),
performance chasing strengthens in the middle and upper ranks, an effect
that is unaffected by the uncertainty control and stable across the 2010
regimes, although it reflects contemporaneous co-movement and does not
survive lagged timing. Flows that chase recent winners more strongly
when optimism is high are flows driven less by expected alpha, since
winner persistence is absent (Section~\ref{sec:persistence}). The
market-based Baker--Wurgler index gives a weaker and different picture:
its point estimates indicate that high sentiment flattens the
flow--performance relation, with losers losing less flow, but the joint
tests are at best significant at 10\% ($p = 0.053$ for
$\text{SENT}^{\perp}$, $0.105$ for $D^{\text{SENT}}$), the effect is
not stable across the 2010 regimes, and it cannot be separated from
macroeconomic uncertainty: once the \textcite{JuradoLudvigsonNg2015}
index is controlled for, the $\text{SENT}^{\perp}$ $p$-value rises from
0.052 on the common uncertainty sample to 0.213 (Section~\ref{sec:sent_or_unc}), so whatever flattening
there is may reflect rational discounting of noisy performance signals
rather than sentiment.

The margin-debt channel (H2) is the friction. It rejects the disposition
prediction in sign.
High-leverage regimes steepen the flow--performance relationship at both
ends, with a loser-segment interaction that is significant at 5\% and
robust to the fixed-effect structure; under one-month-lagged timing it
keeps its sign and is significant at 5\% one-sided ($z = 2.20$),
and with a control for macroeconomic uncertainty it is significant at
